The async state machine struct is heap-allocated via ::FMemory::NewObject<_AsyncSM_*> with memory-tag attribution EMemTag::AsyncStateMachine. The state machine's lifetime is bound via XSharedPtr<_AsyncSM_*> — the caller holds a shared pointer, the continuation lambda captures a shared pointer by value (NOT raw [this] which would dangle if the awaiter is destroyed mid-await). When all shared pointers go out of scope (caller completes + continuation completes), the state machine is destroyed and freed. The XGCRootSpan stays registered as long as the heap allocation is live (i.e., as long as any shared pointer exists).

Iterator (yield return; Q12 resolution) [Rev 2: applied FIX-B-MEDIUM-30]. Same treatment as async: state-machine struct with XGCRootSpan over captures across yield boundaries. Sim-path iterator allocation: iterators ARE allowed on sim-path (unlike async, which is banned) because their semantics are deterministic given deterministic input. BUT the heap-allocation cost per iterator instance is unacceptable on the sim-path frame budget unless mitigated. Sim-path TUs emit iterators with pool-allocated state machines: a per-iterator-shape pool (per-type) pre-allocates N state machines at module load; GetEnumerator() draws from the pool. Pool exhaustion emits warning XIL2CPP085 — sim-path iterator pool exhausted; consider raising pool capacity or restructuring the call site.

// C# input:
//   public IEnumerable<XActor> GetVisibleActors()
//   {
//       foreach (var a in allActors) {
//           if (a.IsVisible) yield return a;
//       }
//   }

// C++ output: state-machine struct with the same XGCRootSpan pattern as async.
// MoveNext returns true if a value was yielded; the yielded value is in the
// state-machine's Current slot.

5.10 Lock and synchronization (Q5 resolution)

Sim-path: BANNED. lock(obj) in a sim-path TU emits XIL2CPP043 — lock(obj) is banned on sim-path TUs (single serial executor; locking is a category error).

Non-sim-path: maps to FScopedCriticalSection over a per-object monitor. Per-object monitors are lazily allocated on first lock; the monitor itself is a 56-byte FCriticalSection from XCore-4a.

// C# input (non-sim-path):
//   lock (someObject) {
//       // critical section
//   }

// C++ output:
{
    ::XCore::Concurrency::FScopedCriticalSection lock(
        ::XCore::Concurrency::FObjectMonitorRegistry::GetOrCreate(someObject));
    // critical section
}

The FObjectMonitorRegistry is a per-process singleton that maintains a thread-safe map from XObject* identity to FCriticalSection. [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-MED-13] The registry keys on XObject::InternalIndex (the FXObjectArray slot) rather than XObjectKey; this avoids the XObjectKey.GetHashCode() sim-path ban (XIL2CPP046) by using a different identity hash entirely. [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-HIGH-9] Monitor cleanup: when a locked object's BeginDestroy lifecycle slot fires (XCoreXObject §2.4 EXObjectLifecycleSlot::BeginDestroy), the runtime invokes an OnXObjectBeginDestroy hook; FObjectMonitorRegistry subscribes to it and removes the entry. Cost: O(1) hash-remove per object destruction. This prevents monitor leak across hot-reload class replacement (where instances of OldClass are destroyed and new instances of NewClass are created with fresh monitors).

5.10.5 Interlocked.* mapping (non-sim-path) [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-HIGH-11 / FIX-B-HIGH-26]

Sim-path: System.Threading.Interlocked.* is BANNED with diagnostic XIL2CPP055 — Interlocked.* is banned on sim-path TUs (single SerialExecutor; no atomic surface needed; replace with plain assignment). Rationale: sim-path is single-threaded; atomic-ness is unnecessary; the C++ emit cost of full memory barriers per Interlocked call is unacceptable. Authors using Interlocked.Increment on sim-path replace with ++counter.

Non-sim-path: Interlocked APIs map to C++ std::atomic<T> operations with sequentially-consistent ordering (matching .NET semantics):

  • Interlocked.Increment(ref counter) → std::atomic_fetch_add(&counter, 1, std::memory_order_seq_cst) + 1.
  • Interlocked.Add(ref counter, n) → std::atomic_fetch_add(&counter, n, std::memory_order_seq_cst) + n.
  • Interlocked.CompareExchange(ref dest, newValue, comparand) → std::atomic_compare_exchange_strong(&dest, &comparand, newValue) with the appropriate read-modify-write semantics.
  • Interlocked.Exchange(ref dest, newValue) → std::atomic_exchange(&dest, newValue, std::memory_order_seq_cst).

The target field must already be declared std::atomic<T> in the C++ emit; if the C# field is plain int (not volatile) and used with Interlocked, XIL2CPP promotes the C++ emit to std::atomic<int32_t> + emits warning XIL2CPP056 — field 'X' used with Interlocked but not declared volatile; promoting to std::atomic<T> in C++ emit. Consider declaring as 'volatile' for clarity.

5.11 Reflection runtime (Q7 resolution)

XIL2CPP supports a restricted reflection surface [Rev 2: applied FIX-B-MEDIUM-27 / FIX-B-MINOR-01]:

  • typeof(SomeClosedType) (statically known) → SomeClosedType::StaticClass() — direct.
  • typeof(SomeOpenGeneric<>) (the open generic) → FindClass("SomeOpenGeneric") returning a "generic-type-definition" FClass that's distinct from closed instantiations.
  • typeof(T) inside a generic method → at the closed-instantiation walk, T is known; emit T::StaticClass() after type substitution. At the unresolved open-generic site, emit XIL2CPP054 — typeof(T) at open-generic site cannot be resolved at compile time.
  • obj.GetType() → obj->GetClass() (the FClass-driven dispatcher returns the object's const FClass*).
  • typeof(T).Name → interned const FString* matching the literal "T" (per Contract §6.2 line 1117-1120).
  • typeof(T).FullName → interned const FString* matching the XPact-canonical format "Namespace::Outer::Inner<T>" (XPact-canonical, not .NET conventional). Nested types use :: separator; closed generics use angle-brackets. Documented in §5.11.
  • (obj as T) / obj is T → ::XCore::Reflect::Cast<T>(obj) / ::XCore::Reflect::IsA<T>(obj); both dispatch through the FClass chain.

Banned [Rev 2: applied FIX-B-HIGH-22 / FIX-B-MEDIUM-32 — Activator.CreateInstance promoted to error on all TUs]:

  • Type.GetMethods(), MethodInfo.Invoke(), Type.InvokeMember() → XIL2CPP050 error.
  • System.Reflection.Emit → XIL2CPP052 error (permanently banned per Locked Commitment 1).
  • System.Activator.CreateInstance(Type t) → XIL2CPP051 error on all TUs (not just sim-path). Rationale: requires runtime type metadata that XPact's pure-transpile model genuinely cannot host without a managed runtime. The diagnostic suggests using XObject.New<T> with a compile-time T.
  • Type.MakeGenericType, Type.MakeGenericMethod → XIL2CPP053 error. Runtime generic instantiation requires a managed runtime; not available in XPact.

5.12 Exception handling (try/catch/finally; throw)

Per Contract §5.6-5.7: try/catch/finally and throw force the enclosing function to Tier 1 (the XResult shim form). The emit differs Dev/Test vs Shipping:

Dev/Test lowering (native exceptions).

// C# input:
//   public void DoWork() {
//       try {
//           SomethingThatMightThrow();
//       }
//       catch (FileNotFoundException ex) {
//           log.Error(ex.Message);
//       }
//       finally {
//           Cleanup();
//       }
//   }

// C++ output (Dev/Test):
extern "C" void _v1..._DoWork_Body(...) {
    try {
        SomethingThatMightThrow_Body(...);
    }
    catch (const ::XCore::Exception::XCSharpException& ex) {
        if (ex.TypeIs<::FileNotFoundException>()) {
            log.Error(ex.Message);
            goto finally_block;
        }
        // Re-throw if no matching catch:
        Cleanup();
        throw;
    }
finally_block:
    Cleanup();
}

Shipping lowering (Result<T, E> early-return).

extern "C" void _v1..._DoWork_Body(::XCore::Exception::XResult* outResult) {
    ::XCore::Exception::XResult inner;
    SomethingThatMightThrow_Body(&inner);
    if (inner.discriminator == ::XCore::Exception::XResult::Discriminator::Error) {
        if (inner.error.code == ::FileNotFoundExceptionCode) {
            log.Error(inner.error.message);
        }
        else {
            // No matching catch; propagate
            *outResult = inner;
            Cleanup();
            return;
        }
    }
    Cleanup();
    outResult->discriminator = ::XCore::Exception::XResult::Discriminator::Success;
}

The XResult RAII destructor. Per Constraint §2.9: XIL2CPP emits the per-call-site destructor teardown for XResult values containing non-trivially-destructible non-XObject types (e.g., FString, TArray<T>). The teardown is emitted automatically at every call site where the function returns an XResult containing such a type.

Exception filters (catch ... when (filter)) [Rev 2: applied FIX-B-HIGH-20]. C# 6+ catch (T ex) when (filter) lowers in the Dev/Test path to:

try {
    SomethingThatMightThrow();
}
catch (const T& ex) {
    if (!(filter)) throw;   // unwind continues
    // handle ex
}

Note: C++ rethrow unwinds; C# filter does not. For the small set of programs that rely on the non-unwinding semantic (e.g., to inspect side effects of unwind-skipping), emit diagnostic XIL2CPP033 — exception filter relies on non-unwinding semantics that differ from C++ catch+rethrow; consider restructuring. In the Shipping XResult path, the filter check is inline in the discriminator branch and no unwinding occurs — no semantic divergence.

try / finally (no catch) [Rev 2: applied FIX-B-HIGH-21; Rev 3 rewrite per FIX-D-HIGH-05 / FIX-D-MED-01]. Lowered via a RAII scope guard so the finally body runs whether the try body throws or completes normally. Two RAII variants exist post-Rev-3 to honor C++'s noexcept contract correctly:

  • XScopedGuard: cleanup body is provably-non-throwing (Pass 4 tier-classified noexcept). Used when the cleanup body is itself noexcept-correct (e.g., trivial flag-clear, atomic counter decrement). C++ destructor marked noexcept; any throw propagation is std::terminate.
  • XScopedGuardWithThrow: cleanup body may throw. C++ destructor is NOT marked noexcept; if the cleanup throws, the standard double-exception handling fires (current-exception-takes-priority); a throw during stack unwinding routes through XCore::HAL::Abort with a clear diagnostic. Used when the cleanup body cannot be proven non-throwing.

Pass 4 tier classification of the cleanup body decides which variant is emitted; if the cleanup body could throw (transitively calls a Tier 1 shim, calls a user method without proven NoThrow), XScopedGuardWithThrow is emitted.

// C# input:
//   try { Body(); } finally { Cleanup(); }

// C++ output (Cleanup proven noexcept; emit XScopedGuard):
{
    ::XCore::Container::XScopedGuard _guard([&]() noexcept { Cleanup(); });
    Body();
}
// _guard's destructor fires on scope exit (both normal completion and exception unwinding).

// C++ output (Cleanup MAY throw; emit XScopedGuardWithThrow):
{
    ::XCore::Container::XScopedGuardWithThrow _guard([&]() { Cleanup(); });
    Body();
}
// _guard's destructor fires on scope exit. If Cleanup throws while the current exception
// is unwinding, XCore::HAL::Abort fires with "double-exception in scope-guard cleanup".

The XScopedGuard + XScopedGuardWithThrow RAII types are part of XPact.CSharp.BCL (forward-commit BCL-Rev2-HANDLER). The same pattern generalizes try/catch/finally:

// C# input:
//   try { Body(); }
//   catch (FileNotFoundException ex) { LogError(ex.Message); }
//   finally { Cleanup(); }

// C++ output (Cleanup proven noexcept; LogError may throw; XScopedGuard for finally):
{
    ::XCore::Container::XScopedGuard _guard([&]() noexcept { Cleanup(); });
    try { Body(); }
    catch (const ::FileNotFoundException& ex) { LogError(ex.Message); }
}
// _guard fires on normal exit AND on exception (it's RAII).

return inside try with finally [Rev 2: applied FIX-B-HIGH-21]. The return value is stored in a local, the scope-end runs the finally via the appropriate scope guard, then the local is returned:

// C# input:
//   try { return ComputeValue(); } finally { Cleanup(); }

// C++ output (assuming Cleanup proven noexcept):
{
    ::XCore::Container::XScopedGuard _guard([&]() noexcept { Cleanup(); });
    return ComputeValue();   // _guard fires before the actual return propagates up
}

XCSharpException surface [Rev 2: applied FIX-B-MEDIUM-23]. The XPact exception type's C# surface mapping:

  • Exception.Message → ::XCSharpString field on XCSharpException.
  • Exception.StackTrace → ::XCore::Container::XStackTraceHandle opaque handle with ToString() stringifier. Backed per-arch by RtlCaptureStackBackTrace + PDB (Win64) or _Unwind_Backtrace + DWARF (Linux/Quest 3 Android). Forward-commit: detailed exception-class spec lives in a separate XException.html doc to be authored post-MVP.
  • Exception.Source / Exception.TargetSite: post-MVP (require additional reflection support).
  • Exception.InnerException chain: supported via the Throw-with-cause API; XCSharpException's body carries an optional InnerException pointer (manually managed by the throw site).

The captureStack() method on XCSharpException invokes the platform helper to materialize an XStackTraceHandle at throw time; symbolication is deferred until StackTrace.ToString() is called.

5.13 stackalloc and Span (Q10 resolution)

stackalloc T[n] emits as alloca-equivalent (compiler-supported stack allocation):

// C# input:
//   Span<int> buf = stackalloc int[16];

// C++ output:
int32_t _stack_buf[16];   // compile-time-sized array on the stack
::XCore::Span::TSpan<int32_t> buf(_stack_buf, 16);

// For dynamically-sized stackalloc:
//   Span<int> buf = stackalloc int[n];
// emits via alloca:
int32_t* _stack_buf = static_cast<int32_t*>(alloca(n * sizeof(int32_t)));
::XCore::Span::TSpan<int32_t> buf(_stack_buf, n);

Span<T> / ReadOnlySpan<T> emit as XPact-defined value-type pair:

template <typename T>
struct TSpan {
    T*       m_data;
    size_t   m_count;
    T*       Data() const { return m_data; }
    size_t   Count() const { return m_count; }
    T&       operator[](size_t i) { return m_data[i]; }
    const T& operator[](size_t i) const { return m_data[i]; }
    TSpan<T> Slice(size_t start, size_t length) const {
        return TSpan<T>{m_data + start, length};
    }
};

Span<XObject*> ban [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-HIGH-13 / FIX-B-MINOR-09]. The ban extends to all Span shapes that directly or transitively contain XObject references. Build error XIL2CPP080 — Span<T> / ReadOnlySpan<T> where T is or transitively contains an XObject reference is not supported in MVP. Banned forms include: Span<XObject*>, Span<XActor>, Span<XPtr<T>>, ReadOnlySpan<XActor>, Span<SomeStructWithXActorField>, Span<Span<T>> with transitive XObject containment. The same rule applies to ReadOnlySpan<T> — the read-only nature does not bypass the lifetime concern. The Span value type itself, when stored on the stack, contributes zero entries to the stack-map (the Span is a view; the rooted body is elsewhere, registered by its owning container).

Span lifetime / ref struct rules [Rev 2: applied FIX-B-MEDIUM-34]. C# enforces Span lifetime rules at the source level via ref struct semantics: Span cannot be stored in heap-allocated objects, captured in lambdas, returned across async boundaries, etc. XIL2CPP relies on Roslyn's CS8347-family diagnostics to reject illegal escape patterns at parse time. Post-XIL2CPP analyzer (gameplay-tier rule extension): XIL2CPP082 — Span<T> escapes its stack frame; ref struct rules violated for the rare cases Roslyn misses.

stackalloc size limit [Rev 2: applied FIX-B-MEDIUM-35; Rev 3 extension per FIX-D-MED-05]. Unbounded alloca is a stack-overflow risk. XIL2CPP emits a runtime size check on dynamically-sized stackalloc:

// C# input:
//   Span<int> buf = stackalloc int[n];

// C++ output:
if ((size_t)n * sizeof(int32_t) > XPACT_MAX_STACKALLOC_BYTES) {
    ::XCore::HAL::Abort("stackalloc size exceeds XPACT_MAX_STACKALLOC_BYTES (64 KB)");
}
int32_t* _stack_buf = static_cast<int32_t*>(alloca(n * sizeof(int32_t)));
::XCore::Span::TSpan<int32_t> buf(_stack_buf, n);

Default XPACT_MAX_STACKALLOC_BYTES = 64 KB (configurable per-module).

Compile-time-sized stackalloc check [Rev 3 per FIX-D-MED-05]. Compile-time-sized stackalloc (stackalloc int[16]) emits no runtime check, but Pass 3 verifies that the static size does NOT exceed the limit at build time. A C# author writing stackalloc int[100_000] would otherwise emit int32_t _stack_buf[100000]; (400 KB stack usage), bypassing the runtime check. Pass 3 adds:

// Pass 3 check (build-time):
//   stackalloc T[N] where N * sizeof(T) > XPACT_MAX_STACKALLOC_BYTES emits diagnostic:
//   XIL2CPP086 — stackalloc T[N] exceeds 64KB stack-allocation limit at static size;
//                 reduce N or use TArray<T> (heap-allocated, RAII-managed).

The diagnostic fires at the build level (not at runtime); authors must address it before the build compiles. This closes the static-size escape hatch the Rev 2 limit advertised but did not enforce.

5.14 Events and delegates

Per Constraint §2.15 + XHT.html §17.4 line 1611, 1615: Action<T> / Func<T> field declarations require explicit [XDelegate] attribute. The Contract §1.1 amendment authorizing this is pending; until then XHT emits diagnostic XHT123 and XIL2CPP defers emit to a post-MVP revision.

[Rev 2: applied FIX-A-MED-27 / FIX-B-MEDIUM-21] When Contract §1.1 lands the [XDelegate] amendment, the delegate emit pattern is:

  • Per closed delegate type (Action<int>, Func<int, string>, etc.), emit a per-type wrapper struct: FXDelegate<ReturnT, Args...> = {XPtr<XObject> target, ReturnT (*invoke)(void*, Args...)} for single-cast; TArray<FXDelegate<...>> for multi-cast.
  • The capture problem (lambda-bound delegate state) is resolved by the display-class pattern (per §5.9 / FIX-B-HIGH-06): the target is the display-class instance; the invoke is the display class's operator().
  • Multicast semantics: delegate += other → emit an immutable shallow copy of the previous array with the new entry appended; delegate -= other → immutable shallow copy with matching entry removed. This matches C#'s Delegate.Combine / Delegate.Remove immutable-collection semantics.
  • GC rooting: For multi-cast delegates, the underlying TArray<FXDelegate<...>> container's XGCRootSpan covers every target XObject*. For single-cast delegates (stored as embedded fields of containing objects), the containing object's schema vector covers the delegate's target XObject*.
  • Invocation: delegate(args) → delegate.Broadcast(args) for multicast, or direct dispatch delegate.invoke(delegate.target.Raw(), args) for single-cast.

Until the Contract §1.1 amendment lands, declaration of Action<T> / Func<T> fields without [XDelegate] emits diagnostic XHT123 (XHT-side) and XIL2CPP defers emit to post-MVP.

5.15 IDisposable and using

Per Constraint §4.7: XObject implements IDisposable with Dispose() calling MarkForKill(). Raw ~XObject() direct calls are forbidden (XIL2CPP090 — explicit destructor call on XObject-derived type; use MarkForKill() or let the GC reclaim).

using block lowers at AST normalization to a Dispose-safe RAII pattern [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-MED-3 / FIX-B-MEDIUM-22]:

// C# input:
//   using (var x = new Disposable()) { ... }

// AST-normalized:
//   var x = new Disposable();
//   try { ... } finally { ((IDisposable)x).Dispose(); }

// C++ output (Rev 3: Dispose-safe scope guard; XScopedGuardWithThrow handles Dispose throws cleanly):
{
    auto x = ::Disposable{};
    // [Rev 3: applied FIX-D-HIGH-05 / FIX-D-MED-01] — use XScopedGuardWithThrow because
    // x.Dispose() is not provably noexcept (Pass 4 cannot guarantee Dispose is non-throwing
    // for arbitrary user-author Disposable types). The guard's destructor is NOT noexcept;
    // double-exception (Dispose throws during stack unwinding) routes through HAL::Abort.
    ::XCore::Container::XScopedGuardWithThrow _disposeGuard([&]() {
        try {
            x.Dispose();
        } catch (...) {
            // Dispose-time exception swallowed to preserve the original exception
            // per the C# language convention. Diagnostic is logged via XLog at Verbose
            // for post-mortem — XLog::Verbose MUST be noexcept (or the catch propagates).
            //
            // Pass 4 verifies XLog::Verbose is noexcept-clean (forward-commit XLog
            // contract); if XLog::Verbose can throw (e.g., OOM on buffer alloc), the
            // outer try-catch (...) here catches THAT too. The catch body itself is
            // straight-line code with no further throwing operations.
            //
            // NET effect: XScopedGuardWithThrow's destructor either completes cleanly
            // (Dispose threw, was swallowed) OR HAL::Abort fires (XLog itself threw mid-swallow).
        }
    });
    ...
}
// _disposeGuard fires on normal exit AND on exception. Dispose runs exactly once.

The single-Dispose-fire property is the load-bearing C# semantic: the original Rev 1 lowering had a subtle bug where Dispose-time exceptions in the catch block would lose the original exception. Rev 2 swallows Dispose-time exceptions (matching C# 8+ using spec). For XObject-derived using targets, Dispose() maps to x->MarkForKill(); the destructor is NOT called; the GC reclaims the slot on next sweep.

using declaration (using var x = ...;) [Rev 2: applied FIX-B-MEDIUM-22]. The C# 8+ using declaration extends through the end of the enclosing scope. The lowering wraps the rest of the scope in the XScopedGuard pattern, NOT a new block scope. Roslyn's lowering already handles this; XIL2CPP follows the rewritten AST.

XObject-derived using targets [Rev 2 clarification per FIX-A-MED-3]. For using (XActor a = factory.Create()) { ... }: Dispose() maps to a->MarkForKill(); the destructor is NOT called at scope-end (XObject is GC-managed; the destructor would be inappropriate). The using block adds a single MarkForKill call at scope-exit. For non-XObject using targets: Dispose() is a member function call; the destructor is the C++ scope-end destructor. Both run — this is consistent with IDisposable's typical RAII contract. If T::Dispose() releases resources that the destructor would also release, the class author must ensure idempotence.

5.16 Checked and unchecked arithmetic (Q8 resolution)

Per Q8 resolution: default semantics is unchecked (standard C++ overflow rules: wraps for unsigned; undefined for signed but matches the underlying architecture's two's complement). The checked block emits explicit overflow checks:

// C# input:
//   int Add(int a, int b) {
//       checked {
//           return a + b;
//       }
//   }

// C++ output:
int32_t Add(int32_t a, int32_t b) noexcept {
    int32_t result;
    if (__builtin_add_overflow(a, b, &result)) {
        ::XCore::HAL::Abort("integer overflow in checked arithmetic");
    }
    return result;
}

The overflow check uses __builtin_add_overflow family (Clang / GCC) or _addcarry_u32 / SafeAdd equivalents on MSVC.

The sim-path commitment: cross-arch behavior MUST be identical. Unsigned overflow wraps the same on x86_64 + ARM64 (per the C++ standard); signed overflow is undefined behavior in C++, but the toolchain matrix is locked in a cross-compiler-safe way. [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-MED-8]

Sim-path signed-overflow policy [Rev 2: rewrite per FIX-A-MED-8]. The Rev 1 design referenced /d2overflow- for MSVC as a provisional flag — that flag is an undocumented MSVC internal switch and is the wrong mechanism. The Rev 2 resolution:

  • Clang / GCC: sim-path TUs add -fwrapv at the compile-step (defined signed overflow as wrap; portable, well-supported).
  • MSVC: no flag needed for current versions (MSVC empirically wraps signed overflow as documented), BUT this is not guaranteed across future MSVC versions and Itanium-ABI commits signed overflow as UB. The future-proof + cross-compiler answer is to emit explicit modular wraps via uint-casts at every signed-int arithmetic operation in sim-path TUs: 
    // C# input (sim-path):
//   int Sum(int a, int b) { return a + b; }

// C++ output (sim-path; explicit modular wrap):
int32_t Sum(int32_t a, int32_t b) noexcept {
    return (int32_t)((uint32_t)a + (uint32_t)b);   // unsigned add wraps; cast back
}

Cost: ~1 extra cycle per signed-int operation. Acceptable on sim-path which already pays for /fp:precise. Non-sim-path TUs continue with C# default unchecked semantics (no wrap guarantee).

5.17 Fixed statement (Q9 resolution)

Per Q9: non-moving GC means pinning is trivially satisfied. fixed emits as a direct pointer assignment:

// C# input:
//   fixed (int* p = &arr[0]) { *p = 42; }

// C++ output:
{
    int32_t* p = &arr.Data()[0];  // arr's data pointer is already stable per non-moving GC
    *p = 42;
}

No pinning machinery is generated. Reading C# code that uses fixed on XObject* is rejected as XIL2CPP091 — fixed statement on XObject reference is meaningless; the GC is already non-moving; the C# author should use unsafe + raw pointer arithmetic if they really need the pointer (with the same XObject* unsafe guards from §5.18).

Pointer-into-XObject lifetime [Rev 2: applied FIX-B-MINOR-08]. The non-moving GC does NOT relocate cells, BUT it CAN free them (mark-and-sweep). A fixed-acquired pointer into an XObject's field (e.g., fixed (int* p = &actor.HealthAmount)) is valid ONLY while the underlying XObject remains reachable. If the GC collects the XObject during the fixed block, the pointer becomes dangling. The C# author MUST keep a strong reference to the XObject in scope (e.g., via a local XActor a = actor;) for the duration of the fixed block. The local is registered in the FStackMapRecord and keeps the GC from collecting. This is documented behavior, not a runtime check.

5.18 Unsafe blocks (Q6 resolution)

Per Q6: unsafe allowed for non-XObject types. XObject* in unsafe context must use stack-map registration via an explicit XGCRoot::AddRoot bracket.

// C# input (non-sim-path):
//   unsafe {
//       int* p = (int*)Marshal.AllocHGlobal(16);
//       *p = 42;
//       Marshal.FreeHGlobal((IntPtr)p);
//   }

// C++ output:
{
    int32_t* p = static_cast<int32_t*>(::FMemory::Alloc(16));
    *p = 42;
    ::FMemory::Free(p);
}

Sim-path TUs ban unsafe blocks entirely (the Marshal.* surface is non-deterministic + the pointer-arithmetic semantics interact with sim-path's strict cross-arch reproducibility). Diagnostic XIL2CPP092 — unsafe blocks are banned on sim-path TUs.

5.19 LINQ ban + post-MVP plan

[Rev 2: applied FIX-B-MEDIUM-31] MVP is LINQ-less on all paths. Sim-path TUs reject any call to System.Linq.* with XIL2CPP100 — LINQ is banned on sim-path; use explicit foreach or TArray.Filter / TArray.Map. Non-sim-path TUs receive the same diagnostic with milder wording: XIL2CPP100 — LINQ is post-MVP; use explicit foreach + TArray helpers (TArray<T>::Filter, TArray<T>::Map, TArray<T>::Reduce) for now. Deferred-execution LINQ operators land post-MVP after careful design. This is a substantial ergonomic regression that authors must plan around; XPact's gameplay-tier guidance documentation will provide migration patterns.

Replacement surface (XPact-canonical):

  • list.Where(p).ToList() → list.Filter(p) on TArray<T> (synchronous; allocates result array).
  • list.Select(f).ToList() → list.Map(f).
  • list.Sum() → list.Sum() (XPact-defined).
  • list.Count() → list.Num().
  • list.First() / FirstOrDefault() → list[0] / list.NumOrDefault(0).
  • list.Any(p) → list.AnyOf(p).
  • list.All(p) → list.AllOf(p).

Post-MVP LINQ design: deferred-execution operators (returning IEnumerable<T>) require an iterator state-machine per chain link; on sim-path the allocation pattern + delegate state-object overhead is unacceptable. The post-MVP plan integrates deferred LINQ with the XPact gameplay-tier task system to amortize allocation; out of scope for MVP.

5.20 Permanently banned features

  • dynamic → XIL2CPP011 — 'dynamic' is not supported; XIL2CPP is a static transpiler.
  • DllImport → XIL2CPP012 — P/Invoke is not supported; use [XExternal] for native C++ calls (post-MVP).
  • System.Reflection.Emit → XIL2CPP052 — dynamic code emission is not supported; XPact has no managed runtime.
  • Thread-creation APIs (Thread.Start, ThreadPool.QueueUserWorkItem, Task.Run) → XIL2CPP013 — direct thread creation is banned; use the XPact task system (XTaskGraph).
  • I/O (System.IO.File, System.Net.Sockets) → XIL2CPP014 — direct I/O is not in the BCL surface; use XPact's asset/network APIs.

5.21 using X = SomeType<int>; alias-any-type (C# 12) [Rev 2: applied FIX-B-CRIT-04]

C# 12 introduces "alias any type": using FlowMap = Dictionary<FName, XActor>; or using Pair = (int, string); declares a file-scoped alias to ANY type, not just named types. (Pre-C#-12, using aliases were limited to named types.) XIL2CPP supports this by emitting a C++ using alias in the file's anonymous namespace (matching the file-scoped C# semantics):

// C# input (file-scoped):
//   using FlowMap = Dictionary<FName, XActor>;
//
//   public class Engine {
//       FlowMap flows;
//   }

// C++ output [Rev 3: applied FIX-D-MED-04 — emit the using alias in .cs.cpp (single-TU),
// NOT in .cs.h (multi-TU). The Rev 2 emit-in-anonymous-namespace-in-header pattern had
// 100-instance duplication across including TUs even though it was technically correct.
// Moving to the .cs.cpp avoids the per-TU duplication and matches the C# file-scoped semantics.

// In HealthPickup.cs.h (header; public surface):
namespace Simgenics::XPact::Game {
    class XEngine {
        // FlowMap is NOT visible at the header level; the field declaration uses the
        // fully-qualified target type so the header doesn't depend on the alias.
        ::XCore::Container::TMap<::FName, ::XCore::Reflect::XPtr<::XActor>> flows;
        // ...
    };
}

// In HealthPickup.cs.cpp (single-TU implementation):
namespace {
    using FlowMap = ::XCore::Container::TMap<::FName, ::XCore::Reflect::XPtr<::XActor>>;
}

// Body code in this .cs.cpp can use the short name FlowMap; other TUs cannot see it.

Tuple-type aliases. using Pair = (int, string); emits the underlying tuple struct (in shared Generated/Tuples.h per FIX-D-MED-12 ODR resolution — see below) + a C++ using alias in the per-TU .cs.cpp:

// C# input:
//   using Pair = (int Year, string Title);

// C++ output (in HealthPickup.cs.cpp):
namespace {
    using Pair = _Tuple_int_XCSharpString;   // tuple struct from shared Generated/Tuples.h
}

File-scope alias correctness [Rev 3 per FIX-D-MED-04]. The using alias lives in the .cs.cpp file's anonymous namespace (single-TU; internal linkage). This is the architecturally-correct location: each .cs.cpp is one TU; the anonymous namespace has internal linkage; the alias does not pollute the cross-TU header surface. The Rev 2 emit-in-.cs.h pattern was functional but mislabeled: every header-including TU got its own local copy of the alias (not multi-TU leakage at the linker level, but IDE-navigation confusion). The Rev 3 emit-in-.cs.cpp form eliminates the confusion entirely.

5.22 Exception class surface [Rev 2: applied FIX-B-MEDIUM-23 forward-reference]

Per §5.12 forward-reference: the detailed exception-class surface (Message, StackTrace, Source, TargetSite, InnerException chain, stack-walking infrastructure) is documented in XException.html (forthcoming, post-MVP). XIL2CPP MVP supports the canonical Exception methods listed in §5.12; deeper reflection on Exception types is post-MVP.

6. GC Integration

XIL2CPP is the largest single contributor to XPact's GC correctness. The collector at runtime (XCoreXObject's mark-and-sweep) relies on XIL2CPP-emitted scaffolding to enumerate roots and locate references. If XIL2CPP misses any of: a stack-resident XObject* local, a container's XGCRootSpan registration, a reference-storing assignment without the write barrier, or a function-entry safe-point check, the collector's correctness is compromised — potentially crashing the game or, worse, silently corrupting state. This section enumerates the emit obligations.

6.1 Precise stack roots: FStackMapRecord per function via SHADOW-STACK mechanism

[Rev 2: rewrite per FIX-A-CRIT-2 / FIX-A-HIGH-12 / FIX-A-MED-30 / FIX-C-HIGH-17] The Rev 1 design proposed "emit explicit register spilling at every safe-point" which is architecturally infeasible portably under the locked toolchain matrix (MSVC + Clang + GCC) — MSVC dropped x64 inline asm in 2008, C++ has no portable surface for forcing specific stack offsets, the optimizer chooses register vs stack independently, and live values can be rematerialized from the spill slot leaving the slot stale. The audit rejected this approach.

Rev 2 mechanism: SHADOW STACK. Every transpiled C# function (including: instance method, static method, virtual dispatcher, constructor, static constructor, operator overload, property getter/setter, indexer accessor, lambda body, async MoveNext, iterator MoveNext, local function) allocates an explicit XPtr<XObject>* _liveRefs[N] array on the stack at function entry, where N is the count of distinct XObject* locals declared in the function. Each captured XPtr local declaration is paired with an emit-time-stable index into _liveRefs; the array slot is stored at the local's declaration site and cleared at end-of-scope. The FStackMapRecord's liveRefOffsets[] lists offsets within the _liveRefs array (which the optimizer CANNOT fold away because it has explicit address-of usage). This is the Unity IL2CPP model and is portable across MSVC + Clang + GCC.

The record format per XCoreXObject.html §5.4 line 1281-1290:

namespace XCore::Reflect {
    struct alignas(8) FStackMapRecord {
        uint32_t pcRangeBegin;     // byte offset within the function (from function entry)
        uint32_t pcRangeEnd;       // byte offset within the function (from function entry)
        uint16_t numLiveRefs;
        uint16_t _pad;
        int32_t  liveRefOffsets[]; // byte offsets within the function's _liveRefs[] array (NOT raw frame offsets)
    };
}

The PC range demarcates the range of program counter values within the function for which the listed live refs are the canonical set. A simple function (no branching) emits one record covering [0, 0xFFFFFFFFu]. A function with conditional locals (e.g., if (cond) { XActor a = ...; }) emits multiple records covering the disjoint PC ranges where the locals are live; each record's liveRefOffsets[] refers to the same _liveRefs shadow-stack array (the array slots not in current scope are cleared to nullptr, so the collector sees no spurious roots).

Long-lived vs short-lived locals. Locals whose lifetime spans a safe-point get stored to _liveRefs at declaration; short-lived locals (no safe-point crossing) get stored only at the last-use boundary. Pass 3 dataflow analysis determines which case applies; Pass 6 emits accordingly.

Per-function overhead: 1 stack slot (8 bytes) per distinct XObject* local + 2-4 cycles per safe-point per live local (stack-store + stack-load). For a function with ~5 live XObject locals, this is ~40 bytes stack + ~10-20 cycles per safe-point. Acceptable per X4 acceptance gate.

Recursive struct walk [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-HIGH-12]. For stack-resident value-type locals (struct / record struct / tuple), Pass 3 performs a recursive walk of the type tree. For each transitive XObject reference field (at any nesting depth), the field is also stored to a _liveRefs slot at the struct's declaration:

// C# input:
//   struct InnerSlot { public XActor Holder; }
//   struct OuterContainer { public InnerSlot inner; public int meta; }
//
//   void DoWork() {
//       OuterContainer container = ...;
//       // container.inner.Holder is a stack-resident XObject ref nested two structs deep
//   }

// C++ output [Rev 3: applied FIX-D-HIGH-02 (ordering) + FIX-D-HIGH-10 (stack-struct barrier semantics)]:
extern "C" void _v1..._DoWork(::SomeObject* self) noexcept {
    // Step (1): Shadow-stack array decl (zero-init) — N=2 covers the UNION of liveness across the
    // function's PC range (self always live; container.inner.Holder live after step (4)).
    // [Rev 4: applied FIX-F-NIT-1 clarification]
    // _liveRefs is a SCAN-ONLY view into live refs, NOT a separately-owned reference.
    ::XCore::Reflect::XPtr<::XCore::Reflect::XObject> _liveRefs[2] = {};

    // Step (1a): self slot write — populates _liveRefs[0] BEFORE any safe-point fires.
    _liveRefs[0] = ::XCore::Reflect::XPtr<::XCore::Reflect::XObject>(
        reinterpret_cast<::XCore::Reflect::XObject*>(self));

    // Step (2): Container constructors (XGCRootSpan registers).
    // For this example, no container locals; this step is empty.
    // (If the function had a TArray local, its constructor would run here BEFORE the safe-point.)

    // Step (3): Safe-point check — now that the shadow stack + container roots are
    // fully populated, the collector can safely observe this frame.
    XPACT_SAFEPOINT_CHECK();

    // Step (4): User body.
    ::OuterContainer container = /* ... */;
    _liveRefs[1] = ::XCore::Reflect::XPtr<::XCore::Reflect::XObject>(
        reinterpret_cast<::XCore::Reflect::XObject*>(container.inner.Holder));
    // Per FIX-D-HIGH-10: stack-resident structs with XObject references update
    // _liveRefs[index] (shadow-stack tracking) but do NOT emit XPACT_GC_STORE
    // because there's no XObject parent for the card-table mark — the parent
    // is the STACK, not an XObject. The collector's mark phase sees the new
    // value via the shadow stack.
    // At end of container's scope: _liveRefs[1] = nullptr.
}

Stack-resident struct write-barrier rule [Rev 3: applied FIX-D-HIGH-10]. For stack-resident structs containing XObject references (e.g., OuterContainer.inner.Holder):

  • Updates _liveRefs[index] to the new value (shadow-stack tracking).
  • Does NOT emit XPACT_GC_STORE because there's no XObject parent for the card-table mark; the parent is the stack, not an XObject.
  • The collector's mark phase sees the new value via the shadow stack (the stack-map record's liveRefOffsets[] includes the embedded ref slot's offset within _liveRefs).

Pass 3 enumeration walks struct-with-ref fields and marks them as shadow-stack-only writes, not barrier writes; Pass 6 emits accordingly. The struct-with-ref enumeration recurses to any nesting depth so nested struct fields (e.g., container.inner.deeper.Holder) are correctly tracked.

Safe-point ordering at function entry [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-HIGH-12]. Function-entry safe-point fires AFTER any container/RAII-managed-root constructors complete and BEFORE the function body's first user statement: (1) shadow-stack init → (2) container constructors (XGCRootSpan registers) → (3) safe-point check → (4) user body. This ordering prevents the GC from observing a partially-constructed safe-point view.

Size budget per XCoreXObject.html §5.4 line 1292: ~30 bytes per function; ~300 KB for 10k transpiled functions. Cache layout: all FStackMapRecord live in a single .rodata section, indexed by function start address. The collector at safe-point time does a binary search for the running function's start address.

FStackMapRecord emit example.

// C# input:
//   public void DoWork() {
//       XActor a = this.GetActor();
//       XComponent c = this.GetComponent();
//       a.Tick(0.016f);
//       c.Update();
//   }

// C++ output:
extern "C" void _v1..._DoWork(::SomeObject* self) noexcept {
    // Shadow stack: self + a + c = 3 slots
    ::XCore::Reflect::XPtr<::XCore::Reflect::XObject> _liveRefs[3] = {};
    _liveRefs[0] = ::XCore::Reflect::XPtr<::XCore::Reflect::XObject>(
        reinterpret_cast<::XCore::Reflect::XObject*>(self));
    XPACT_SAFEPOINT_CHECK();

    ::XActor* a = self->GetActor();
    _liveRefs[1] = ::XCore::Reflect::XPtr<::XCore::Reflect::XObject>(
        reinterpret_cast<::XCore::Reflect::XObject*>(a));
    ::XComponent* c = self->GetComponent();
    _liveRefs[2] = ::XCore::Reflect::XPtr<::XCore::Reflect::XObject>(
        reinterpret_cast<::XCore::Reflect::XObject*>(c));

    a->Tick(0.016f);
    c->Update();
    // End of scope clears _liveRefs[1] and _liveRefs[2].
}

// FStackMapRecord (in .rodata):
static const ::XCore::Reflect::FStackMapRecord _StackMap__DoWork = {
    /* pcRangeBegin */ 0,
    /* pcRangeEnd   */ 0xFFFFFFFFu,
    /* numLiveRefs  */ 3,
    /* _pad         */ 0,
    /* liveRefOffsets[] */ { 0, 8, 16 }  // offsets into _liveRefs[] (NOT raw stack offsets)
};

// Registration with the collector [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-MED-30]:
// XStackMapTable lazy-init in XCoreXObject Rev 5 means we can safely call Register
// at any static-init time; no init-order hazard.
extern "C" int _StackMap_DoWork_RegisterAtLoad = [](){
    ::XCore::Reflect::XStackMapTable::Register(
        reinterpret_cast<uintptr_t>(&_v1..._DoWork),
        sizeof(_v1..._DoWork),
        &_StackMap__DoWork);
    return 0;
}();

The function-size-at-link-time computation is supported on all three toolchains (MSVC: __ImageBase + linker-emitted symbol size; Clang/GCC: __builtin_function_size or the linker's .size directive).

Generic-method principal-TU rule [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-HIGH-5; Rev 3 precision per FIX-D-LOW-01]. For closed instantiations of a generic method, the FStackMapRecord is emitted in the principal TU (the lexicographic-by-NFC-normalized-Unicode-codepoint-first source file where the closed instantiation is referenced). NFC normalization ensures cross-OS deterministic ordering for Unicode module names (the same accented module-name byte sequence sorts identically on Windows and Linux filesystems). Other TUs that re-emit the same instantiation by accident are linker-safe via __attribute__((selectany)) (Clang/GCC) / __declspec(selectany) (MSVC) on the registration object. This prevents double-registration with the collector.

Per-platform shadow-stack [Rev 2: applied FIX-C-HIGH-17]. The shadow-stack mechanism is platform-NEUTRAL by design (the array of pointers is portable C++; offsets are array-relative not register-relative; the optimizer can't fold the array because of address-of usage). Win64 and ARM64 produce identical shadow-stack layouts; per-platform FStackMapRecord emit is unnecessary. This is one major win of the shadow-stack approach over the Rev 1 register-spill approach.

6.2 Container roots: XGCRootSpan emit

Per Constraint §2.3 + Contract §3.1 line 547-575: every transpiled container type that may hold XObject references embeds an XGCRootSpan member at the end of the class body. The container's constructor registers the span; resizing updates it; the destructor unregisters it.

Selection rules per Constraint §3.2:

  • Generic arg statically XObject-derived (List<XActor>, Dictionary<FName, XComponent>) → XGCRootKind::Strong.
  • Generic arg statically value type (List<int>) → NO GC scaffolding (pure RAII).
  • Generic arg object or non-statically-typed interface that could hold XObjects → XGCRootKind::Conservative + build-time warning XIL2CPP070 on sim-path TUs.

The container is move-only per Contract §3.4 line 665-699: copy-constructor and copy-assignment are = delete. Move construction/assignment transfer span-registration ownership atomically with respect to the mark phase (the GC's mark phase has a snapshot mechanism per XCoreXObject.html §4 that handles the transfer).

XIL2CPP emits container = true on the class-descriptor flag per Contract §3.7 line 759. XLiveCoding's patch-validation pass reads this flag to enforce the layout-freeze invariant on hot-reload.

Thread-safety [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-LOW-5]. XGC_RegisterRootSpan / XGC_UnregisterRootSpan / XGC_UpdateRootSpan are thread-safe per XCoreXObject Rev 4 §5.3: internal locking covers the registry. Contention is low in practice because container constructors are infrequent relative to container access; the steady-state overhead is dominated by the per-write barrier (not the span registration). For multi-threaded scenarios (non-sim-path), XGCRootSpan registration calls are safe to invoke concurrently from any thread.

6.3 Write barriers: XPACT_GC_STORE emission

Per Constraint §2.6: every reference-storing assignment emits XPACT_GC_STORE. [Rev 2: rewrite per FIX-A-CRIT-5] The Rev 1 detection rule walked only AssignmentExpressionSyntax, missing ~12 distinct reference-store paths. The Rev 2 enumeration is a POSITIVE list of every C# construct that writes a reference slot:

Reference-store sites enumeration [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-CRIT-5]. A reference-store site is any AST node N such that N either is:

  1. An AssignmentExpressionSyntax whose resolved LHS is an FObjectProperty / FWeakObjectProperty / FStructProperty-with-ref (recursive) / FArrayProperty-with-ref / FMapProperty-with-ref / FSetProperty-with-ref.
  2. OR one of the following lowered forms produced by Pass 2 normalization:
    • Object-initializer setter (e.g., new Slot { Holder = actor }) — the per-property setter calls.
    • Collection-initializer Add (e.g., new TArray { actor1, actor2 }) — the Add(item) calls (already covered by TArray's own internal barrier; no caller-site emit needed).
    • Compound-assignment lowering (obj.RefField += newRef, ??=) — the final reassignment to the field.
    • with-expression property mutation on records / structs — the synthesized clone-then-setter calls; the setter fires AFTER copy ctor returns and the new object IS reachable; barrier required.
    • Deconstruction-assignment (e.g., (actor1, actor2) = SwapActors()) — the implicit two per-target assignments.
    • Indexer-setter call (dict[key] = actor) — the TMap's internal barrier; no caller-site emit needed.
    • Auto-property setter backing-field write — the setter body's write to the backing field. Semantic model unwraps backing field to property; the barrier fires at the setter body.
    • Init-accessor backing-field write — same as setter.
    • Default-interface-property setter dispatch — the dispatched concrete-class setter emits the barrier internally; the call site does NOT emit a barrier (the concrete setter doesn't know parent_obj from the caller's perspective; see FIX-A-HIGH-7).
    • Primary-constructor parameter-to-field init — record primary ctors emit barrier; class primary ctors capture into private mutable fields (not reflectable) and emit barrier same as regular field init.
    • Container Add / Set / Remove methods — internally implemented with the barrier; no external emit at the call site.
    • Span<T> indexer setter (span[i] = value) [Rev 3: applied FIX-D-HIGH-09] — Span<T> is a value-type {T*, size_t} per §5.13. If T is XPtr<XActor>, the write IS a reference store and would need the barrier — but the Rev 2 emit ban (XIL2CPP080 banning Span over XObject references) makes this moot in MVP. Enumerated here for completeness; if Span<XActor> ever ships post-MVP, the barrier emit site is the indexer setter on the underlying T* slot.
    • Ref-typed return value assignment (ref var slot = ref obj.field; slot = newValue;) [Rev 3: applied FIX-D-HIGH-09] — C# 7+ ref returns. If slot is a ref to an XObject reference field, the assignment IS a reference store. Banned via XIL2CPP005 / FIX-A-CRIT-3 for XObject element types (the callee cannot recover the correct parent_obj for the SATB pre-store + card-table mark); for non-XObject ref-types, no barrier emit. Pass 3 enumeration tracks ref-returns to identify the underlying owner for diagnostics.
    • Fixed-pointer writes (fixed (T* p = ...) { *p = x; }) [Rev 3: applied FIX-D-HIGH-09] — for T being XObject* (or XPtr<T>): BANNED via XIL2CPP091 (fixed statement on XObject reference is meaningless; the GC is already non-moving) plus an implicit ref-store barrier-bypass concern. For non-XObject T* (e.g., int*, float*), the *p = x assignment does NOT trigger any barrier emit because the slot is a value primitive. For XPtr<T>* pointer dereference (extremely rare; would require explicit unsafe + raw cast), the assignment WOULD bypass the barrier — banned via XIL2CPP091's broader ref-storage scope (the fixed diagnostic covers all XObject-ref pointer arithmetic uniformly).

Pass 3 enumeration walks both AST shapes: direct AssignmentExpressionSyntax with reference LHS AND the lowered forms from Pass 2 normalization. Pass 6 emits XPACT_GC_STORE(parent_obj, &slot, new_value) at each site.

Interface property setter dispatch [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-HIGH-7]. When the LHS is an interface property (e.g., holder.CurrentActor = newActor; where holder is IInventoryHolder), the call site does NOT emit a barrier. The dispatched concrete-class setter is responsible for the barrier, using the correct concrete self as parent_obj. Every C# property setter on a reference type emits XPACT_GC_STORE(self, &_BackingField, value) at the start of the setter body, regardless of whether the property is declared on a class or an interface (default implementation). Default-implemented interface property setters: the default impl emits the barrier; the implementing class either inherits the default (no override emit) or overrides (its own emit).

Read sites do NOT emit a read barrier. Per XCoreXObject.html §5.2: the SATB pre-store barrier model requires only a write barrier; reads are bare pointer loads.

When the barrier is omitted [Rev 2: clarification per FIX-A-MED-11 / FIX-A-MED-12]. Three cases where XIL2CPP intentionally omits the barrier:

  1. New-object initialization — pre-escape-publish. When the assignment occurs inside the object's constructor BEFORE the object's address has been escape-published (no this-pointer leaks to external code, no reference stored to a static, no return to caller), the barrier is omitted. The mark bit on the new object is set to the current mark cycle so it cannot be missed; the bare store is safe. The omission applies ONLY in this pre-escape-publish window. Specifically: with-expression setter calls fire AFTER the copy constructor returns and the new object IS reachable; barriers MUST fire. Object-initializer setters within new Foo { ... } are part of construction; barrier omitted. Setters called after the constructor returns: barrier required.
  2. Within an XCS_OPAQUE region. A future optimization (post-MVP) lets authors annotate a code region as "I promise no GC can run here" via XCS_OPAQUE_BEGIN() / XCS_OPAQUE_END(). Within the region, the barrier is omitted. Out-of-scope for MVP; flagged here as a forward hook.
  3. Stack-local pointer chase intermediate. obj.a.b.c.Field = x: the intermediate reads of obj.a, obj.a.b, obj.a.b.c are bare loads (no barrier; reads have no barrier in any case). The final assignment ... = x emits the barrier. The barrier's parent_obj is the directly-enclosing object (the holder of the Field slot), not the chain root.

ref/out XObject ban [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-CRIT-3]. The Rev 1 design left write-barrier behavior undefined for ref XObject / out XObject parameters — the callee cannot know the correct parent_obj for the SATB pre-store + card-table mark. Rev 2 BANS ref/out XObject reference parameters in MVP (diagnostic XIL2CPP005); see §5.2. Authors use return-value-based APIs (XActor? TryGetActor(int id)) or Result<T, E>.

Memory-ordering forward commitment [Rev 2: FIX-DEFERRED-Rev3 per FIX-A-HIGH-10]. The Rev 1 XPACT_GC_STORE macro uses a plain (non-atomic) slot store after the SATB pre-store barrier. On x86_64 (Total Store Order), this works; on ARM64 (weakly ordered), the SATB queue push and the slot store can be reordered by the CPU, allowing the marker thread to see the new value before the old value lands in the SATB queue. The correct fix requires Contract Rev 14 coordination with XCoreXObject Rev 5 to add memory-order discipline: the SATB load uses std::memory_order_acquire; the slot store uses a release-fence sequencing. Forward-commit: XPACT_GC_STORE_MEMORY_ORDER_TAG ABI tag locking the cross-arch acquire/release pattern. The Rev 2 emit is the same shape as Rev 1; the per-arch fences land with XCoreXObject Rev 5.

6.4 Outer-chain emit (Locked Commitment 3 enforcement)

Per Locked Commitment 3 + §5.3: every XObject.New<T>(outer, name, flags) call site has an explicit Outer. XIL2CPP enforces this at emit time:

  1. Pass 3 verifies that every XObject.New<T> call has all three arguments at the call site (Roslyn's overload resolution catches missing arguments at parse time, but XIL2CPP double-checks for defensive depth).
  2. Pass 3 verifies that the outer argument is NOT null (a literal null in the source emits diagnostic XIL2CPP002 — XObject.New<T> called with null Outer; pass an actual owning XObject).
  3. Pass 6 emits the C++ NewObject<T'>(outer, name, flags) directly; XCoreXObject's runtime path asserts outer != nullptr as belt-and-suspenders.

Note: bona fide null-outer use cases (root objects of an ownership chain) must use the explicit XObject.NewRoot<T>(name, flags) factory which has no outer parameter and is restricted to internal engine use via an [XObjectInternalConstructor] attribute. Gameplay-author C# cannot call it. This pattern matches UE's NewObject<T>(Outer=GetTransientPackage(), ...) default but makes the omission explicit at the type level. [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-LOW-8] Forward commitment: XObject.NewRoot<T> requires XCoreXObject Rev 5 to publish the corresponding NewRootObject<T>(Name, Flags) C++ runtime entry point in its §3.5 NewObject contract. Rev 2 commits to the C# surface; the C++ runtime side lands with XCoreXObject Rev 5.

6.5 Safe-point checks at function entry and back-edge

Per Constraint §2.5: every XIL2CPP-emitted function entry inserts a function-entry safe-point check; every long-loop back-edge inserts a back-edge safe-point check.

The safe-point macro [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-CRIT-6 / FIX-A-LOW-4 / FIX-A-LOW-6]:

// In XCoreXObject/Internal/XSafePoint.h (forward-commit: XCoreXObject Rev 5 publishes this contract):
//
// Uses TLS-cached pending-flag per XCoreXObject Rev 4 §5.5 lines 1296-1307.
// The TLS read + branch is ~2 cycles in steady state.
//
#define XPACT_SAFEPOINT_CHECK() \
    do { \
        /* [Rev 3: applied FIX-D-MED-06] — C++20 [[unlikely]] attribute attaches to */ \
        /* the if-statement, NOT inside the condition expression. The position */ \
        /* `if (cond) [[unlikely]] { ... }` is the spec-conformant form per */ \
        /* [dcl.attr.unlikely]. The Rev 2 form `if ([[unlikely]] cond)` was */ \
        /* C++ syntax error (attribute on expression is not accepted). */ \
        if (::XCore::Reflect::XGCSafepoint::s_tlsPendingFlag) [[unlikely]] { \
            ::XCore::Reflect::XGCSafepoint::HandlePending(); \
        } \
    } while (0)

// BACKEDGE alias [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-LOW-4] — currently identical to entry-check;
// provisioned for future divergence (back-edge polling may be cheaper than entry polling
// for tight loops; the alias allows a future optimization without changing call sites).
#define XPACT_BACKEDGE_SAFEPOINT_CHECK() XPACT_SAFEPOINT_CHECK()

The cost is ~2 cycles per call in steady state per XCoreXObject.html §5.5 line 1307 (one TLS-cached read + one branch when no safe-point is pending). [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-LOW-3 — corrected from Rev 1's incorrect "3-5 cycles" citation, which actually applied to the write barrier, not the safe-point check.]

Macro lowering origin [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-CRIT-6]. The Rev 1 design INVENTED XPACT_SAFEPOINT_CHECK and XGCSafepoint::s_pendingFlag without referencing actual XCoreXObject contracts. Rev 2 forward-commits XCoreXObject Rev 5 to publish the canonical surface:

  • Macro name: XPACT_SAFEPOINT_CHECK() defined in Engine/Source/Runtime/XCore/Public/XSafePoint.h.
  • TLS variable: ::XCore::Reflect::XGCSafepoint::s_tlsPendingFlag (1-byte; per-thread; updated by a fan-out broadcast when the GC requests safe-point).
  • Pending-handler: ::XCore::Reflect::XGCSafepoint::HandlePending().
  • ABI tag: XPACT_SAFEPOINT_FLAG_ABI_TAG in the Contract surface (Rev 14 addition) so cross-tool drift is detected.
  • Memory-order: TLS access is per-thread, so atomic ordering is local to the thread; cross-thread visibility uses an acquire/release pair when the GC publishes the safe-point request. The TLS-local check is intentionally relaxed-equivalent.

This commit closes the Rev 1 gap where XPACT_SAFEPOINT_CHECK would fail to compile against the current XCoreXObject.

[[unlikely]] usage [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-LOW-6]. Rev 1 used XPACT_UNLIKELY which is an XPact-invented macro not documented in any other XPact doc. Rev 2 uses the C++20 [[unlikely]] attribute directly. C++20 is the locked output language; the attribute is supported by all three toolchains (MSVC 19.44+, Clang 17+, GCC 13+).

Long-loop detection rule. Pass 3 analyses every for, while, do/while, and foreach loop. The loop is "long" if:

  1. The iteration count is unbounded by static analysis (the loop's iteration count is not a compile-time constant or trivially-bounded variable), OR
  2. The iteration count is a compile-time constant > 16 (the default threshold; configurable per-module via SafePointLoopThreshold in the ModuleRules).

If the loop is long, the back-edge safe-point check is emitted. If the loop is provably short (compile-time constant ≤ 16), the back-edge check is omitted.

Critical design decision (safe-point coverage). The GC's mark phase relies on every running function reaching a safe-point in bounded time. If any function or any loop bypasses the check, the GC's mark phase can hang indefinitely. Per XCoreXObject.html §5.5: the coverage guarantee is “100%”. Round 1 audit MUST verify that no XIL2CPP emit path omits the entry-safe-point check. The corollary: XIL2CPP-emitted code MUST NOT call C++ functions that loop unboundedly without their own safe-point check; this is enforced at Tier 1/Tier 2 classification (Pass 4 marks any callee that's not XPact-internal as potentially-blocking and forces the caller to a Tier 1 shim with a safe-point check at shim entry).

6.6 Conservative root scanning for List<object>

When the C# container's generic argument is object or a non-XObject-derived interface that could hold an XObject (e.g., IDamageable), XIL2CPP cannot statically prove whether the contents are XObjects. The container emits an XGCRootKind::Conservative span; the collector treats every slot as a potential XObject* and validates at scan time (the slot is dereferenced as XObject* only if it's a valid pointer into the XObject heap).

Sim-path TUs emit warning XIL2CPP070 — Conservative XGCRootSpan used; cross-arch determinism may be affected. Consider replacing object with a typed reference. The warning is per-closed-type-instantiation, deduplicated via the XIL2CPP build cache.

Conservative validation order [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-MED-16]. Per XCoreXObject Rev 4 §5.3 ("Conservative validation order per FIX-A-MED-35"): the collector treats every slot value as a potential XObject* and validates at scan time using a pointer-range check against the FXObjectArray's known address range. If in range, treat as XObject*; if out of range, skip. Fast (~2 cycles per slot). XIL2CPP-emitted code for List<object> must produce slots whose values are either valid XObject* or known-safe-non-XObject-values; arbitrary unmanaged data through List<object> would misidentify and crash. Per the new FIX-B-CRIT-06 ban (List<object> with value-type entries is banned via XIL2CPP073), the misidentification risk is reduced; only XObject and explicit boxing-wrapper types are accepted.

Conservative-on-sim-path policy [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-MED-29]. Per Contract Rev 13.9 §4.5 line 885: SimPathConservativeRootsAllowed is a per-target setting. If false (default for strict sim-path builds), XIL2CPP070 is ELEVATED FROM WARNING TO ERROR on sim-path TUs (build exit 41). If true (relaxed sim-path), XIL2CPP070 stays as warning. XIL2CPP reads the setting from the per-module manifest at Pass 3.

Dedup cache layout [Rev 2: applied FIX-C-MED-13]. Per-module dedup cache at Intermediate/Build/<Target>/<Configuration>/<Module>/Transpiled/ConservativeWarnings.json:

  • Cache key: the closed-type-instantiation full name (e.g., "List<object>", "Dictionary<FName, IDamageable>").
  • Cache value: {firstReportFile: string, firstReportLine: int, count: int}.
  • Consultation: at warning emit time; if the key is already in the cache, the warning is suppressed (deduped within the module's transpile run).
  • Cross-module: NO dedup. The same List<object> in M1 and M2 emits twice (once per module). This is correct because warnings are per-module-of-occurrence.
  • Invalidation: cache invalidates when the module's .cs sources change (matches action-cache discipline).

6.7 Struct-with-XObject-field handling

A C# struct that contains an XObject reference field is handled per storage:

  • Stack-stored. The function's FStackMapRecord registers the byte offset of the struct's XObject field (relative to the stack-frame).
  • Class-field. The enclosing class's schema vector recurses via EXObjectRefSchemaOp::Struct opcode.
  • Array-stored. TArray<TStruct> with an XGCRootSpan partial specialization that recurses; the stride accounts for the struct's size; the GC walks each array element's XObject field as a root.

6.8 GC integration acceptance gates

Per XCoreXObject.html §13.2 + Constraint §9.2:

  • X10: precise stack scanning enumerates 100% of XIL2CPP-emitted XObject locals on Win64 + Quest 3 in a synthetic test with 100 nested transpiled functions, each with 1-5 stack-resident XPtrs.
  • X-DET: every sim-path TU SerialNumber/XObjectKey access guarded.
  • X-NEW: sim-path NewObject runtime invariant fires from non-SimPathSerialExecutor.
  • X-SCHEMA: schema-vector walk > 4000 refs/ms on Quest 3.
  • X4: write barrier emit cost ≤ 5 cycles.

6.9 Memory-tag attribution [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-MED-33]

XIL2CPP-emitted code that calls FMemory::Alloc, FMemory::Realloc, XCore::Reflect::NewObject attributes memory to specific tags:

  • XObject allocations: EMemTag::XObject (XCore-4a default).
  • Sim-path container allocations: EMemTag::SimPathContainer (new tag; forward-commit XCore-4a Rev 5).
  • Lambda / display-class allocations: EMemTag::Lambda (new tag).
  • Async state machine allocations: EMemTag::AsyncStateMachine (new tag).
  • Iterator state machine pool allocations (sim-path): EMemTag::IteratorPool (new tag).
  • Boxing wrapper allocations: EMemTag::Boxing (new tag).

Memory leak tracking distinguishes sim-path tier allocations from editor-tier; the leak tracker reports per-tag size at frame-end.

6.10 Bitfield ordering for emitted types [Rev 2: applied FIX-C-MED-17]

XIL2CPP-emitted bit-packed types (records, structs that contain flags) use explicit uint32_t flag storage with manual bit operations, NOT C++ unsigned int : N bitfields. Rationale: bitfield ordering is implementation-defined; cross-arch consistency (Win64 LSB-first; ARM64 nominally LSB-first but implementation-allowed to be MSB-first) is not portable. By avoiding C++ bitfields, XIL2CPP emit is cross-arch identical.

A static check at module init: static_assert(IsLittleEndian()) per target. (All XPact-locked targets are little-endian.)

7. Sim-path TU Discipline

7.1 Module-level sim-path declaration

A module is sim-path iff its descriptor declares sim_path = true in TOML or SimPath = true in .Build.cs per Contract §4.1 line 781. The flag propagates to all TUs in the module; per-file overrides are not supported in MVP. If a file legitimately needs sim-path semantics in an otherwise-non-sim-path module, the file must be split into its own micro-module.

XIL2CPP reads the flag from the per-module manifest (Module.sim_path: bool; Constraint §7.6).

7.2 Compile flag enforcement

XBT forces the following compile flags on sim-path TUs per Contract §4.2 line 811-839 (excerpts):

  • MSVC: /fp:precise; banned /fp:fast, /fp:except.
  • Clang Linux/Win: -ffp-contract=off, -fno-fast-math, -fno-finite-math-only, -mno-fma; banned -ffast-math, -funsafe-math-optimizations, -ffp-contract=fast/on, -mfma.
  • Clang Android ARM64: same + -mllvm -enable-fp-contract=false.
  • SIMD constrained to ≤ SSE42.
  • PCHUsage = NoSharedPCHs (forced).

XIL2CPP does NOT directly emit these flags (XBT propagates them to the C++ compile step). [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-CRIT-4 — the Rev 1 design referenced XPACT_FP_SEMANTICS_TAG as a static_assert, but Contract Rev 13.9 §10.2 line 1984 explicitly excludes FPSemantics from the manifest schema; no such ABI tag exists.] Sim-path FP semantics are enforced by XBT's per-target flag derivation at the compile step itself (banned flags per Contract §4.4 fail the C++ compile loudly). For belt-and-braces compile-time guarding, XIL2CPP emits a check against predefined compiler macros:

// Belt-and-braces guard: detect fast-math activation via compiler-defined macros.
#if defined(__FAST_MATH__) || defined(_M_FP_FAST)
#  error "sim-path TU compiled with fast-math; banned per Contract §4.2; rebuild with /fp:precise (MSVC) or omit -ffast-math (Clang/GCC)"
#endif

Clang/GCC define __FAST_MATH__ when -ffast-math is active; MSVC defines _M_FP_FAST when /fp:fast is active. Sim-path requires neither. The check is per-.cs.cpp, so a wayward fast-math flag fails every sim-path TU's compile.

MSVC /fp:precise vs /fp:strict rationale [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-MED-7]. Sim-path uses /fp:precise (NOT strict). Rationale: /fp:strict enables IEEE 754 exception handling on denormal operations which is unnecessarily slow on sim-path. /fp:precise is sufficient: it disables fma contractions (banned per Contract §4.2), disables fast-math rewrites (banned), and keeps IEEE-754 correctness for non-exception operations — matching the Sleef-vendored-transcendental discipline.

7.3 Runtime checks on sim-path

XIL2CPP emits runtime checks at the call sites per XCoreXObject.html §3.5 and friends:

Call siteRuntime check emittedSource
XObject.New<T>XPACT_CHECK_SL(IsSimPathThread() || !IsSimPathTU())XCoreXObject.html §3.5 line 845
obj.GetSerialNumber()XPACT_CHECK_SL(!IsSimPathTU())XCoreXObject.html line 301
obj.MarkForKill()XPACT_CHECK_SL(!IsSimPathTU(), "MarkForKill is non-sim-path; next-GC-sweep timing is non-deterministic")XCoreXObject.html line 329-330
XObjectKey.GetHashCode()XPACT_CHECK_SL(!IsSimPathTU())XCoreXObject.html line 341
FXObjectArray.Iterate orderedXPACT_CHECK_SL(!IsSimPathTU())XCoreXObject.html line 2956 fix

The XPACT_CHECK_SL macro in Dev/Test mode asserts (with file:line + custom message) and aborts via XCrashReportClient; in Shipping it remains compiled in (per XCoreXObject.html §2 line 301: “a compiled-in guard active on sim-path TUs”) and aborts directly.

7.4 Build-time banned-API enforcement

XIL2CPP maintains a banned-API list (XSimPathBannedAPIs.txt) read at Pass 3 semantic analysis. Every InvocationExpressionSyntax and MemberAccessExpressionSyntax in a sim-path TU is checked against the list. Matches emit diagnostic XIL2CPP040 — sim-path TU calls banned API <FullName> with build exit 41.

The Rev 1 list (additive to Contract §4.4's preprocessor-level regex check):

  • System.DateTime.Now, System.DateTime.UtcNow, System.DateTime.Today.
  • System.Environment.TickCount, System.Environment.TickCount64.
  • System.Diagnostics.Stopwatch (all members).
  • System.Random (all members); System.Random.Shared.
  • System.Threading.Thread.CurrentThread.ManagedThreadId.
  • System.Threading.Thread.Sleep, System.Threading.SpinWait.
  • System.Threading.Tasks.Task.Run, Task.Delay.
  • System.Threading.ThreadPool.*.
  • System.Console.* (output ordering depends on host buffer flush).
  • System.IO.File.* (filesystem access timing is non-deterministic).
  • System.Net.* (network access timing).
  • System.Guid.NewGuid.
  • System.GC.* (programmatic GC trigger is non-sim-path).
  • System.Runtime.InteropServices.Marshal.*.
  • System.Linq.* (allocation pattern + deferred-execution unpredictability).
  • Reflection-based Activator.CreateInstance, Type.GetMethods, MethodInfo.Invoke.
  • object.GetHashCode() on reference types (default hash code is identity-based; unstable across runs).

The list is augmented per-Contract-revision; Rev 13.9's banned-API regex (Contract §4.4) covers the C preprocessor side. XIL2CPP's list catches the C# IL2CPP-source side before the preprocessor sees the emit.

Rev 2 additions [applied FIX-A-HIGH-2 / FIX-A-HIGH-11 / FIX-B-HIGH-22]:

  • System.Threading.Tasks.Task<T> field / param declarations → XIL2CPP048.
  • System.Threading.Tasks.ValueTask<T> → XIL2CPP048.
  • System.Threading.Tasks.Task.Result property access → XIL2CPP048.
  • System.Threading.Tasks.Task.Wait() → XIL2CPP048.
  • System.Collections.Generic.IAsyncEnumerable<T> → XIL2CPP044 / XIL2CPP048.
  • System.Threading.Interlocked.* → XIL2CPP055.
  • System.Activator.CreateInstance → XIL2CPP051 (Rev 2 elevated to error on all paths, not just sim-path).
  • System.Type.MakeGenericType / System.Reflection.MethodInfo.MakeGenericMethod → XIL2CPP053.

7.8 Sim-path determinism mapping table [Rev 2: applied FIX-A-HIGH-3]

Cross-arch bit-exact determinism requires every potentially-non-deterministic C# surface to map to a deterministic emit. The mapping table:
